# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-23

## RAG

| **[Operon: Incremental Construction of Ragged Data via Named Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16080v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern data processing workflows frequently encounter ragged data: multidimensional collections whose inner lengths vary and are revealed only at runtime. Ragged data is prevalent in domains throughout science and computing, yet existing workflow engines treat it as nested containers. This representation leaves shape semantics ambiguous, obscures task dependencies when upstream coordinates are not yet enumerated, and imposes coarse synchronization barriers that stall independent work across tasks. We argue that ragged data is fundamentally a flat function over a coordinate space, and that making this view explicit resolves all three difficulties at once. We realize this insight in Operon, a statically verifiable workflow engine built around a formalism of named dimensions with explicit dependency relations. Users declare pipelines in the Operon DSL by annotating data flow with dimension names, where the static checker verifies that every task does not include faulty iterations regardless of runtime lengths. At runtime, the system models in-progress arrays as partial functions over downward-closed coordinate domains, which provides a natural representation for partially-unknown data states, an exact notion of per-task indegree before all upstream coordinates are enumerated, and a sound basis for fault recovery without programmer intervention.</p></details> |  |
| **[From prompting to evidence-based translation: A RAG+prompt system for Japanese-Chinese translation and its pedagogical potential](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03387v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models perform well on high-resource pairs but are less reliable for Japanese-Chinese sentences containing noun-modifying clause constructions (NMCCs). This study evaluates a retrieval-augmented generation RAG+Prompt translation system that integrates linguistic analysis, embedding-based retrieval, prompt construction, and LLM generation without modifying the base model. The analysis module outputs A1 (inner vs. outer NMCC) and A2 (risk predictions: lexical choice/NMCC handling/word order/style/register); top-k = 5 similar Ja-Zh examples (L2 distance) and A1/A2 are inserted into an enhanced prompt. Using GPT-4o and a 66-sentence test set, we compare six knowledge-base sizes (0/100/200/500/1,000/2,000). Macro-averaged sentence-level BLEU (1-4-gram with brevity penalty; cased; Chinese at the character level) is the sole metric. Mean BLEU increases from 24.28 at 0 (RAG disabled) to 29.96 at 2,000 (+5.68; +23.4%). The upward trend holds across sizes, with larger knowledge bases yielding higher scores. We conclude that the RAG+Prompt translation system improves Ja-Zh translation of sentences containing NMCCs in an interpretable and auditable manner. Limitations include one base model, one metric, and reliance on published texts and commercial APIs; future work will broaden genres, language pairs, and evaluation metrics.</p></details> | <details><summary>The a...</summary><p>The authors have identified errors in the experimental data and have decided to withdraw this version of the manuscript</p></details> |
| **[SIREN (Luring LLMs onto the Rocks): PAIR-Driven Preference Manipulation in Web-RAG Recommenders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21951v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper investigates the adversarial manipulation of the ranked recommendations produced by web-augmented large language models (LLMs). When an LLM answers a recommendation query by retrieving and reading live webpages, it acts as a recommender, and each retrieved page becomes a potential attack surface. Prior work has examined fabricated products, retrieval poisoning, and rank promotion. However, these studies do not compare how different edits to an already retrieved page change the model's final ranking while the surrounding source set remains unchanged. To address this gap, we propose SIREN, an automated attacker--judge method that adapts the PAIR jailbreaking loop to competitive rank manipulation, with the goal of moving a chosen entity to rank~1 in an LLM-generated recommendation. SIREN retrieves and captures webpages using Anthropic's web tools, then iteratively edits a retrieved source using an interpretable taxonomy of 23 content-poisoning techniques. The custom-RAG replay platform keeps the same sources in the same order, so changes in the model's ranking can be linked to changes in the supplied content rather than to differences in retrieval. Across two production Claude models, SIREN reaches rank~1 in 62 of 124 technique trials nested within eight query--model contexts. The payloads that reached rank~1 were then tested in fresh sessions, where they reproduced the result with a mean success rate of 0.805. Across the evaluated settings, declarative ranking claims and seeded lists were generally more effective than directive-form injections, although the strength of this difference depended on the target model. To the best of our knowledge, this is among the first controlled studies of competitive rank manipulation in production LLMs where the supplied source context is kept fixed.</p></details> |  |
| **[Context-Aware Prompt Injection Detection via Small Language Models in RAG Pipelines: nokast-secureRAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28364v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Local Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are vulnerable to indirect prompt injection and retrieval poisoning, where malicious instructions in retrieved content are mistaken for trusted input. We present nokast-secureRAG, a roughly 0.5B-parameter small language model that acts as a semantic firewall between retrieval and generation. It jointly evaluates the user query and retrieved context, classifying the pair as safe, suspicious, or malicious. The model is trained using a teacher-judge-student pipeline: a 35B teacher generates 5,233 examples across four attack families, and an independent 120B judge re-labels them, achieving 95.0% agreement before student fine-tuning. On a held-out in-distribution test set, the model achieves 0.994 detection recall at a 0.026 false-positive rate and a 0.006 attack-success-rate proxy, outperforming a regex baseline, a public prompt-injection classifier, Llama Guard 3 1B, and its untuned version. Prompt-Guard-86M reaches 0.900 recall but has a 0.690 false-positive rate. On a judgment-flip test, where the same sentence is benign or malicious depending on context, the model correctly classifies both cases 75% of the time, outperforming the compared baselines. Ablations indicate that retrieved context carries the main attack signal, while query-context consistency reduces false positives. Single-request latency is 293-404 ms, above the target of 100 ms, while amortized throughput latency is 37 ms under batching. On the external PoisonedRAG benchmark, recall falls to near zero, indicating that fluent fact-poisoning attacks lie outside the detector's instruction-consistency threat model. We report this limitation alongside the in-distribution results. The model, data-generation pipeline, and evaluation harness are released open source.</p></details> |  |
| **[DeclineCraft: A RAG Abstention Benchmark for Jointly Measuring Combined Retrieval and Parametric Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28340v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) requires recognizing when neither retrieved documents nor parametric knowledge supports an answer. We introduce DeclineCraft, a dynamic abstention benchmark whose unanswerable questions target Minecraft feature additions after a corpus cut-off, with audits of possible parametric exposure. Later cut-offs support evaluation of future models. Across 27 LLMs, we measure Correct Abstention Rate (CAR; how often models acknowledge insufficient knowledge on unanswerable questions), Answerable Attempt Rate and Answer Accuracy. We find that (1) single-shot retrieval significantly raises CAR in 38 of 40 configurations (model and thinking setting) versus no retrieval. (2) Iterative RAG, in which the model self-assesses its context and can request up to two further retrieval rounds, significantly lowers CAR versus single-shot RAG in 30 of 40 configurations (mean 46.4%), with little change in Answer Accuracy (68.5% to 69.3%). Adding query rewriting and reranking to hybrid (dense & sparse) retrieval lowers CAR for all 12 models tested. (3) Larger models answer more accurately (Spearman $ρ= +0.60$ with total parameters), but neither model size nor release date shows a significant association with CAR. (4) Without retrieval, enabling thinking on the same weights lowers CAR significantly for 8 of 13 models (by 14% points on average), and the responses shift from acknowledged uncertainty to premise denial.</p></details> | <details><summary>38 pa...</summary><p>38 pages, 28 figures, 12 tables. Code: https://github.com/Thakur-Atharva/declinecraft-benchmark</p></details> |
| **[Probe, Don't Prompt: A Hidden-State Probe for Metadata Filtering in Multi-Meta-RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03929v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-Meta-RAG improves retrieval for multi-hop question answering by filtering a vector store on metadata, specifically the news source and the publication date, which it extracts from each query using GPT-3.5. This extractor requires a proprietary API call for every query, and its output is not restricted to the allowed sources: even though the prompt specifies 49 sources, GPT-3.5 generates 128 distinct source names, 79 of which do not appear on the list. We show that the source part of this extractor can be replaced by a local, deterministic probe trained on the hidden states of a small open-source language model. The probe is a multi-label logistic classifier on the mean-pooled hidden states of one layer, trained with a class-balanced loss on the gold evidence sources. Using 5-fold cross-validation to select the layer, we evaluate four models ranging from 135M to 1.5B parameters out-of-fold across all 2556 MultiHop-RAG queries. The probe reaches a set-exact accuracy of 90.9%, compared to 88.0% for a substring baseline and 80.9% for GPT-3.5. The difference comes from null queries, for which GPT-3.5 never predicts an empty source set. Across the remaining queries, all three methods achieve set-exact accuracies within 1.1 percentage points of each other. Unlike earlier probing work, we use the probe as a component of a retrieval pipeline rather than for interpretation. For all four models, the cross-validation selects one of the first layers, in contrast to the middle layers reported for retrieval tasks. We attribute this to the source name appearing verbatim in 95.4% of gold query-source pairs. The 135M model is 1.5 percentage points less accurate than the 1.5B model, so the filter requires only a partial forward pass through a small model and one linear layer. The code is available at https://github.com/mxpoliakov/Multi-Meta-RAG.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Computer Science and Applied Mathematics</p></details> |
| **[RAG-NIMBF: A Non-Invasive, Modular Benchmarking Framework for Automated RAG Configuration Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27816v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has become the dominant paradigm for grounding the outputs of large language models (LLM) in external knowledge. However, practitioners are faced with a vast configuration space comprising chunking, embedding, retrieval, reranking, prompting and generation models, and there is limited guidance on how these components interact. We present RAG-NIMBF, a modular benchmarking framework that attaches to existing RAG pipelines as a non-invasive evaluation layer. It evaluates the configuration space through controlled ablation with end-to-end experiment tracking. The evaluation combines LLM-based answer quality scores, retrieval correctness measures, and runtime indicators. This study validates the framework across multiple generation models on the SQuAD dataset. This process reveals non-obvious interactions and demonstrates how the experiments are tracked. The findings indicate that the retrieval balance of relevance and diversity substantially enhances the relevance and coverage of retrieved context in comparison to a conventional similarity search, with MMR raising nDCG@5 by +4 to +11 percentage points across the swept models. More detailed prompt instructions meaningfully increase how faithfully generated answers stick to the retrieved evidence, especially for capable and reasoning models (+10 to +13pp on Qwen3.6-27B, GPT-4o-mini and Qwen3.5-think, but negligible on the 20B generator). Overall, RAG-NIMBF transforms the configuration space of RAG into reproducible, comparable experiments. By attaching to existing production pipelines in a non-invasive way, it enables practitioners to systematically evaluate and improve deployed systems without disrupting existing workflows. This replaces ad hoc choices with evidence-based decisions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Iterate Until Retrieved: Factual Nugget Optimization for Discoverable Continual Corrections in Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25641v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems in business-to-business settings may often receive free-form response feedback. Rather than generic feedback signals such as style, preference, or overall response quality, we focus on actionable factual corrections. We identify these instances and convert them into compact knowledge-base entries, which we call factual nuggets. We introduce Iterative Nugget Optimization (INO), an index-time optimization method that uses the production agentic RAG as a test harness: it creates an initial nugget, probes it with the triggering query and paraphrases, reflects over failed retrieval and answer traces, and revises the nugget until it is discoverable. We evaluate INO with two production B2B knowledge-assistance agents across multiple companies that use our system: a product support agent that answers questions over company-specific knowledge bases, and a support ticket agent that assists support engineers. INO consistently improves results over baselines in terms of discoverability and usage of factual corrections, in automated and human evaluations. A controlled experiment in production shows that the nuggets improve user satisfaction with responses.</p></details> |  |
| **[Large-Scale Biomedical RAG: Corpus Scaling, Document Representation, and Efficiency Trade-offs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26339v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present a controlled, large-scale evaluation of how corpus scale, document representation, and reranking affect the effectiveness and efficiency of biomedical Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems. Retrieval collections were progressively expanded while retaining the relevant source documents, enabling consistent comparisons across corpus sizes. We compared abstract, flat full-text, and hierarchical full-text representations across first-stage retrieval, reranking, and end-to-end multiple-choice question answering. Configurations selected on development data were evaluated on five external biomedical benchmarks and a held-out test set, while retrieval latency and index storage were measured to quantify operational cost. Performance patterns observed with small or controlled collections did not consistently persist as collections grew, and the usefulness of reranking depended on the corpus and retrieval setting. RAG improved three of four model configurations across the external benchmarks, with mean gains of up to 9.6 percentage points, and improved all four configurations on the held-out test set by 10.2-30.3 points. Abstract retrieval performed best when reference evidence was abstract-aligned, whereas full-text representations were generally more effective when richer evidence was needed. Hierarchical representation matched or outperformed flat full-text retrieval in most end-to-end comparisons while requiring 67% fewer indexed units and 44-56% less storage. These findings show that corpus scale should be treated as an explicit evaluation variable in biomedical RAG, and that the most appropriate design depends on evidence format, model behavior, and operational constraints.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 4 figures, 3 tables, plus 11 pages of supplementary material. Preprint submitted to Elsevier. Code: https://github.com/hitz-zentroa/biomedical-rag-scaling and https://github.com/hitz-zentroa/qa-to-mcqa</p></details> |
| **[StrucChunk: Structure-Aware Retrieval for Legal Document RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26313v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) can ground LLM outputs in domain-specific knowledge, but legal documents are hard: standard chunking fragments hierarchical argument structure, and generic embeddings struggle with both precise citation matching and semantic understanding. We propose StrucChunk (Structure Aware Chunking) with hybrid dense-sparse retrieval for hierarchically structured legal documents. It treats documents as directed acyclic graphs of provisions, preserves clause boundaries, resolves cross-references before embedding, and combines BM25 with BGE-M3 via Reciprocal Rank Fusion. We evaluate on five corpora, three legal traditions and two languages. StrucChunk reaches 100% boundary precision (vs. 7.7% for recursive splitting on CrPC), preserves 97.6% of documented cross-references (vs. 0%), and matches section coverage with 40.3% fewer chunks on GDPR. On LegalBench-RAG (6,858 query-passage pairs), it achieves Char-Recall@8 of 48.18% vs. 23.59% for the best baseline (+104%), surpassing the published state of the art by +83%. On 501 queries each for CrPC and CPC (Indian law), Recall@5 improves from 0.557 to 0.804 (+44.4%) and from 0.158 to 0.259 (+64.6%); chunking accounts for 88% and 73% of the gain. On 501 GDPR queries, Recall@5 is 0.868 with R@1 improving +59%. On NitiBench-CCL (3,730 Thai financial law queries), HR@5 is 0.856 vs. 0.594 for naive chunking (+44.1%), showing that section-boundary alignment is the dominant factor across languages, and cross-reference augmentation adds +2.3% on cross-reference queries. We release the implementation, 5,256 evaluation queries across five legal corpora, annotation methodology and chunking quality metrics. Code: github.com/SinghSuryaDeep/legal-rag-strucchunk-1</p></details> |  |
| **[Trust Before Fusion: QIMG-7 and Source-Aware Resolution for Polluted Multimodal RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10798v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is often evaluated with clean evidence, yet real retrieval can return topically relevant but unreliable content: false text and misleading images from corrupted metadata, entity swaps, typographic overlays, semantic edits, adversarial patches, blends, or style transfer. We introduce QIMG-7, a controlled benchmark for multimodal retrieval pollution in multi-sentence factual QA, spanning four datasets, seven image-attack families, and 16 paired clean/polluted regimes, for 1,760 evaluation rows per method. Across four generator/gate stacks, naive multimodal fusion is brittle: in the main gpt-4o-mini stack, Full-MM support drops from 0.908 with clean text to 0.490 with polluted text, often making Parametric fallback safer than retrieval. We propose source-aware trust resolution (SATR), a training-free approach that compares Parametric, Text-only, and Full-MM candidate answers and selects among candidate answers or falls back based on source reliability. The Field-Selector variant achieves the best balanced score, 0.816, improving over Full-MM by 11.7 points and over the Cascaded Router by 2.7 points. Ablations show that, in this text-first setting, explicit text-reliability modeling is the dominant driver of these gains. Overall, in text-first factual QA with multimodal retrieval conflict, our results support selective trust rather than unconditional fusion. Artifacts are available at https://github.com/SaadElDine/Trust_Before_Fusion.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 6 figures, 23 tables. Preprint under review</p></details> |
| **[The Bottleneck Is Retrieval, Not Reading: Diagnosing Evidence-Based Generation with RAG for Long Context Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26951v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is the dominant paradigm for grounding large language models in external corpora, but its similarity-driven, chunked pipeline has structural blind spots: it cannot anticipate which passages a question requires and discards document-level context that resolves cross-passage references. We introduce evidence-based generation (EBG), requiring models to return an answer with line-level citations, and a joint Ans+Evid metric that scores correctness and grounding simultaneously. We reformulate two corpora into evidence-grounded multi-document QA benchmarks- MultiHop-RAG and QASPER-MP, a multi-paper adaptation of QASPER with realistic short paper cues, and pair them with a controlled synthetic two-hop haystack and an evidence-augmented NoLiMa spanning 10K-1M characters. Across ten proprietary frontier and three open-weight LLMs, EBG isolates two RAG limitations that long-context models avoid: similarity retrieval surfaces any relevant chunk far more reliably than all required chunks for multi-fact queries, and chunking destroys document meta-information needed to resolve identity-bearing queries. EBG also exposes grounding failures invisible to answer-only evaluation, including incomplete citations in two-needle settings and a widening gap between Ans and Ans+Evid when context contradicts parametric knowledge. Data and code are available at https://github.com/debobanerjee/LC-EBG.</p></details> |  |
| **[CAT-RAG: Improving Secure Code Generation with Category-Aware Agentic Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24907v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite rapid progress in LLM-based code generation, security vulnerabilities remain widespread, and retrieval-augmented code generation (RACG) can further amplify risk when retrieved knowledge is verbose or vulnerable to poisoning. Existing secure RACG approaches inject security knowledge at a fine-grained vulnerability level, causing prompt overhead and diluted model attention when a query implicates multiple related weaknesses. We propose CAT-RAG, an agentic security category-aware retrieval-augmented code generation framework. CAT-RAG performs adaptive category selection via an LLM as a categorizer agent and operates either as a standalone security layer or as a drop-in add-on to RACG pipelines, requiring no modification to the underlying model. We replace CWE-level retrieval with compact, language-specific security categories. These categories provide a higher-level abstraction over individual CWE weaknesses, organizing them by the security concern they share, such as Input Validation and Injection Attacks, rather than merely grouping related entries. Each category consolidates best practices and secure code examples into a single, prompt-efficient representation. We perform a comprehensive evaluation across CyberSecEval, CodeGuard+, and HumanEval. On CyberSecEval, CAT-RAG nearly doubles the secure code generation improvement over the state of the art (23.57% vs. 12.11%) while reducing token overhead by 41.71%.</p></details> |  |
| **[RAG Strategies for Natural Language-Based SQL Query and REST API Call Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07086v3)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise software systems commonly expose functionality through relational databases and REST APIs, requiring users to understand schemas, endpoints, and parameters. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can support natural language interfaces by grounding large language models (LLMs) in enterprise-specific documentation. We evaluate three training-free RAG configurations - Standard RAG, Relevance Filtering RAG, and Iterative Retrieval RAG - for SQL query generation, REST API call generation, and a combined task requiring routing between both operation types. Using SAP Transactional Banking, we constructed an expert-reviewed dataset with mock execution screening and compared the configurations under database-only, API-only, and mixed-documentation settings. Without retrieval, exact match accuracy is 0% across all tasks, whereas retrieval achieves mock execution success of up to 79.30% and component match accuracy of up to 78.86%. In the combined task, Iterative Retrieval RAG achieves higher exact match accuracy than Standard RAG (10.29% vs. 7.45%), with similar mock execution success (68.93% vs. 67.10%). However, for REST API call generation under mixed documentation, Iterative Retrieval RAG achieves lower mock execution success than Standard RAG (50.53% vs. 75.79%). Overall, retrieval-control strategies show task- and metric-dependent behavior under mixed-documentation settings.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in the IEEE Proceedings of the 3rd International Conference on Foundation and Large Language Models (FLLM 2026), Barcelona, Spain, November 17-20, 2026</p></details> |
| **[VisR-RAG-Fin: Benchmarking Multimodal RAG over Visually Rich Financial Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24822v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise documents such as financial reports are increasingly processed by automated systems, yet they are primarily designed for human reading and exhibit complex visual structure, combining dense text with tables, charts, diagrams, and rich layouts. These characteristics pose significant challenges for Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems, which must retrieve relevant pages and leverage visual and structural information during answer generation. Despite recent advances in multimodal retrieval and vision-language models, there remains a limited understanding of how design choices across retrieval and generation components affect end-to-end RAG performance on visually rich documents. In this paper, we present a comprehensive empirical study of multimodal RAG for page-level Question Answering (QA) over financial documents. We introduce VisR-RAG-Fin (Visually Rich RAG for Finance), an open-source benchmark constructed through a general pipeline for visually rich documents that preserves original layout and visual content. Using this benchmark, we systematically analyze retrieval and generation behavior under text-only, multimodal, and hybrid configurations, providing a foundation for rigorous evaluation of RAG systems on complex enterprise document collections. Overall, this work provides actionable insights for practitioners designing and evaluating multimodal RAG pipelines over complex, visually rich enterprise documents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the main research track of ACM SIGIR-AP 2026, Singapore, December 13-15, 2026</p></details> |
| **[RAGit: Turning Document Preprocessing into an Experimental Variable for RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24659v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Document preprocessing choices can substantially affect retrieval in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipelines, yet they are often selected heuristically and are difficult to compare under consistent conditions. We present RAGit, an open-source experimentation workbench for the systematic and reproducible evaluation of document preprocessing choices through downstream retrieval. RAGit represents parsing, chunking, indexing, retrieval, and evaluation as configurable experimental stages, enabling controlled comparisons while the remaining downstream settings are kept fixed. It preserves provenance from retrieved chunks to source pages and maps heterogeneous retrieval units to a common page-level ranking for evaluation. The current implementation supports multiple parsers and chunking strategies, dense and late-interaction retrieval, reusable intermediate artifacts, and multi-configuration benchmarking. We demonstrate RAGit through controlled experiments comparing alternative preprocessing configurations under a fixed retrieval setup, illustrating how preprocessing can be treated as an explicit experimental variable. RAGit and its documentation are publicly available on GitHub (https://github.com/OmarElbachyr/ragit). A demonstration video is available at https://youtu.be/tsst-Bhfz4I.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages, 2 figures, 4 tables. Accepted at the Tool Demonstration Track of the 33rd Asia-Pacific Software Engineering Conference (APSEC 2026)</p></details> |
| **[REAP: Enhancing RAG with Recursive Evaluation and Adaptive Planning for Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.09966v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has been extensively employed to mitigate hallucinations in large language models (LLMs). However, existing methods for multi-hop reasoning tasks often lack global planning, increasing the risk of falling into local reasoning impasses. Insufficient exploitation of retrieved content and the neglect of latent clues fail to ensure the accuracy of reasoning outcomes. To overcome these limitations, we propose Recursive Evaluation and Adaptive Planning (REAP), whose core idea is to explicitly maintain structured sub-tasks and facts related to the current task through the Sub-task Planner (SP) and Fact Extractor (FE) modules. SP maintains a global perspective, guiding the overall reasoning direction and evaluating the task state based on the outcomes of FE, enabling dynamic optimization of the task-solving trajectory. FE performs fine-grained analysis over retrieved content to extract reliable answers and clues. These two modules incrementally enrich a logically coherent representation of global knowledge, enhancing the reliability and the traceability of the reasoning process. Furthermore, we propose a unified task paradigm design that enables effective multi-task fine-tuning, significantly enhancing SP's performance on complex, data-scarce tasks. We conduct extensive experiments on multiple public multi-hop datasets, and the results demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms existing RAG methods in both in-domain and out-of-domain settings, validating its effectiveness in complex multi-hop reasoning tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>publi...</summary><p>published in AAAI 2026</p></details> |
| **[Upgrade the Retriever First: What an LLM Call per Chunk Buys in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23533v4)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many RAG pipelines pay one LLM call per chunk at indexing time to add titles, summaries, keywords and questions (enrichment) or document context (contextual retrieval, CR). We ask what that call buys over free structure and a better retriever. Our advice: upgrade the retriever first, chunk at headers, and enrich mainly for lexical retrieval. Under a plan made public before any index was built, 7B enrichment beats a free section-path prefix under a hybrid retriever by +5.5 recall points on Qasper; on FreshStack its gain (+2.7) does not survive Holm adjustment. It is equivalent to CR, which sends 5.8-8.7 times the input tokens, and Qasper answer F1 (7B reader) is equivalent for both contrasts. Header-led chunks beat 512-character windows; on FreshStack they lose to overlapping 256-token windows. Under qwen3-embedding and a reranker, the two strongest of eight retrievers (named as secondary before any outcome was computed), the CIs of 7B enrichment against the free prefix fall within the equivalence margins; under BM25 it gains +8.7. Exploratory analyses attribute the hybrid gain to document expansion and to repairing an untuned rank fusion; the free prefix under either strong retriever beats hybrid enrichment by +3.4 to +6.3 points, though the best 120B-indexed configuration leads the best free one by +2.5 on Qasper. Add-ons built on the call do no better than LLM-free controls, and late chunking lowers Qasper recall. We release the plan, harness and per-question results.</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages (8-page main text). v4: confirmatory study with a plan made public before any index was built (951 Qasper and 235 FreshStack questions, eight retrievers). Results of v1-v2 are withdrawn (Appendix P). Code and data: https://github.com/bhavik-mangla/MDKeyChunker</p></details> |
| **[Enhancing RAG-based Medical VQA with Gated Multimodal Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23588v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical Vision-Language Models (Med-LVLMs) have shown strong performance in Medical Visual Question Answering (MVQA), yet they may still produce factually inconsistent responses. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) can mitigate this issue by incorporating external clinical evidence; however, retrieval is often guided by a single modality, potentially overlooking the joint semantics of medical images and clinical questions. We introduce a multimodal RAG framework that learns a joint query representation through a Gated Multimodal Unit, enabling evidence retrieval to leverage information from both modalities while keeping the underlying Med-LVLM frozen. We evaluate the proposed approach on benchmark datasets. Results show that the multimodal query representation consistently outperforms a reference Med-LVLM and remains competitive with state-of-the-art methods. Our findings demonstrate the potential of multimodal query representations for more effective evidence retrieval in Medical VQA.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint submitted to ECIR</p></details> |
| **[RIT-RAG: Navigating Document Corpora with Retrieval-Induced Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11370v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds language models in external corpora. Rather than ranking methods by $\varepsilon$ alone, we propose a framework that interprets a DP-RAG guarantee along three dimensions: privacy granularity, accounting scope, and release boundaries. | **[Copying Is Not Grounding: Training Faithful Long-Form RAG with Authored-Sentence Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18442v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In search engines and assistants, long-form RAG answers can mislead readers with claims their sources do not support. GRPO, the standard RL algorithm, makes it worse by giving every sentence its answer's advantage, reinforcing copied and grounded sentences alike. We propose Authored-Sentence Credit (ASC) , which fixes both by moving GRPO's reward and advantage from the answer to the sentence level. | **[Post-Generation Verification Dominates Retrieval Optimization: A 2^4 Factorial Ablation of RAG Pipeline Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35774v3)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern RAG pipelines stack many enhancement features, but these features are typically validated in isolation, leaving their interactions unmeasured. | **[Training-Free BioNEL-RAG: Advancing Biomedical Entity Linking through Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14789v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biomedical Named Entity Linking (BioNEL) is the task of mapping mentions extracted from biomedical texts to canonical entities or concepts in the standardized knowledge base. We report a deployment case study of a simpler alternative: a portable skill consisting of a natural-language instruction, lightweight tools (a retrieval API, format-checking scripts), and a corpus enriched with an LLM-generated navigation layer, which is a structured "Wikipedia-style" index over the underlying documents. Our claim is scoped to that setting: a mature, well-curated corpus, a latency-tolerant workflow, and human review of every case. | **[ORCAGen: Orchestrating Context-Aware Malware Deception with RAG-Guided Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12415v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Malware defenses often remove or isolate suspicious programs as quickly as possible. We evaluate ORCAGen across GPT-4o, GPT-5.5, Gemini 3.5 Flash, Qwen3-Coder, and Claude Sonnet 4.5 using execution success, hallucination rate, refinement effort, deception effectiveness, and runtime overhead. Across the evaluated scenarios, GPT-5.5 required the fewest refinements and produced no observed hallucinated APIs, while Gemini 3.5 Flash achieved the lowest response time and runtime overhead. We measure misleading rate (MR) on each model's subset of questions answered correctly without retrieval, alongside clean accuracy on the full evaluation set. Instructions that prioritize documents consistently produce higher MR than those permitting reliance on prior knowledge. Averaged over models and positions, the gap ranges from 10.9 to 13.5 percentage points across the three QA datasets. Mean MR follows End $>$ Beginning $>$ Middle under both policies, although individual models do not uniformly follow this ordering. A separate paired audit of 500 questions and two checkpoints supports increased harmful override without establishing a corresponding improvement in beneficial correction. | **[RAGWarrant: Evidence-Preserving Governance for RAG Policy Promotion Under Quality, Cost, Latency, and Risk Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34179v1)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation systems are extensively instrumented with metrics, benchmarks, traces, and automated judges, but these tools do not decide whether a proposed policy change is safe to release. | **[Agentic World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30136v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent world action models provide visual foresight and future prediction for robot manipulation, but lack explicit mechanisms for compositional skill reuse. Agentic robotics combines code-based planning and reasoning with reusable skills, but often lacks predictive grounding in physical dynamics. We introduce AW-0, an agentic world action model framework that integrates code-based planning with joint video and action prediction. We jointly model code, future video, and robot actions as three coupled generative streams within a unified mixture-of-transformers architecture. Within this architecture a code expert generates parameterized skill routines and control logic while video and action experts generate future visual states and low-level motor commands. Code serves two complementary roles: it structures multi-step prediction and control within the world model and represents skills as reusable programs for subsequent agentic execution. We further introduce an automated curation pipeline that labels robot and human video trajectories with aligned programs and consolidates recurring routines into reusable skill libraries. Evaluations on RoboTwin 2.0 and physical bimanual manipulation tasks show improved task performance over VLA and WAM baselines, alongside gains in language following, semantic reasoning and robustness, demonstrating that agentic code enables connection between planning, prediction and execution within WAMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://agentic-wam.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[World of SWECraft: Agent Collaboration at Scale via Simulated Software Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24061v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As teams of AI agents take on larger projects and long-standing open problems, agent collaboration offers another dimension for scaling inference-time compute. It comes with a pipeline to create environments of real, interdependent Python packages of different topics, sizes, and dependency structures, which we call software worlds. In a simulation, agents maintain packages in the world with a high-level goal of improving software efficiency, and they are free to discover tasks to work on and collaborate with others. We evaluate agents' work in a world collectively by measuring speedup in hidden dependent packages. The framework allows us to study different elements of collaboration by varying communication, ownership, and resource allocation. We run simulations in three different worlds, totaling more than 10,000 hours of agent work across eight models. Overall, performance improvement scales with inference-time compute, with the best model improving downstream efficiency by up to 31%. We show that most agents can find meaningful tasks via collaboration, but some frontier models can prematurely stop looking for improvements and make little progress. We release the simulation framework and traces to support future studies of agent collaboration at https://szj.io/sw.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, 9 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |

| **[Privacy Preference Controls for Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29929v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In response to vast and widespread online tracking, regulators and technologists alike have worked to develop laws and tools that allow people to express their privacy preferences. | **[TabAgent: Distilling LLM Agent Components into Cheap Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16429v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic systems, AI architectures that autonomously execute multi-step workflows to achieve complex goals, are often built using repeated large language model (LLM) calls for closed-set decision tasks such as routing, shortlisting, gating, and verification. We propose the Secret-Use Delegation Protocol (SUDP), in which a requester proposes a canonical operation, the user authorizes it with a fresh authenticator-backed grant, and a custodian redeems the grant to perform the bounded use; reusable authority never crosses the requester boundary. We specialize SUDP for LLM-driven agents, where it applies whenever a tool call would exercise user-enrolled authority-bearing material. Under standard cryptographic assumptions, SUDP satisfies six of the seven properties; the seventh, runtime-memory confidentiality, is closed by composition with a hardware-rooted runtime. | **[Agentic Music Post-Production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29093v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce MixAgent, an agentic pipeline for music post-production that follows the workflow of audio engineers to build interpretable and editable mixing plans guided by a reference song. To refine the mix, a detector identifies what needs correction, and a critic proposes numerical parameter adjustments from tracks to the full mix. In our experiments, prompting recent multimodal models to listen and revise often worsens the mix, making reliable correction a central challenge. We therefore build MixCritic, a dataset of 45,657 repair critiques derived from parameters fitted to professional mixes, and train the critic with supervised fine-tuning followed by preference optimization on rendered audio outcomes. On MedleyDB, learned refinement reduces error by 19% relative to the initial mix, with preference optimization reducing it by 8.4% over supervised training alone. Retrieved examples further improve effects selection, and a human listening test shows that MixAgent gets higher quality ratings than mixes produced by Qwen3-Omni, Gemini 3.8 Flash and GPT-4o-audio.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Atomic to Agentic: Towards Interpretable Evaluation of LLMs' Agentic Mathematical Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26950v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are evolving from performing end-to-end mathematical reasoning to integrating agentic intelligence. To bridge this gap, we present a process-level benchmark designed to evaluate the inherent agentic mathematical reasoning abilities of LLMs. Our framework aligns problem-solving agentic behaviors with a structured taxonomy of reusable mathematical atomic capabilities. We design a comprehensive suite of planning, action, and feedback tasks across both textual and multimodal contexts, supported by an automated pipeline that synthesizes high-quality trajectories and produces fine-grained annotations via controlled LLM rewriting. Experiments reveal that models with similar end-to-end accuracy can exhibit markedly different agentic capability profiles. | **[Long-MDR: Long-Context Reinforcement Learning for Multimodal Deep-Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05195v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The next generation of multimodal research agents must reason over long-lived research histories rather than short model completions. Scaling to this regime exposes several practical limitations of conventional RL training. Later, policy entropy can collapse before performance has saturated, prematurely ending useful learning. We introduce Long-MDR, a three-component training recipe designed specifically for this setting: On-Policy Distillation Warmup, Progressive Horizon Expansion, and Entropy-Triggered Rescue. | **[SFT Leaves a Reproducible Signature in Activation Geometry: Calibrating Post-Training Interpretability Against Run-to-Run Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17093v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A model diff credits post-training with whatever separates a base checkpoint from one post-trained descendant, presuming a rerun would land close by. On the sparse co-activation graph of Qwen3-1.7B it does not: two LoRA-SFT reruns sit over half as far apart as either sits from the base. We score each base-to-post contrast against the run-to-run band of its own recipe, and show that $k$ runs certify no better than $p=1/(k+1)$ under run exchangeability, however large the effect, so the band $z$-score is a descriptive scale. SFT leaves a large, reproducible signature: base $\to$ SFT displaces the graph by 17.8 pairwise-distance SDs beyond the mean distance between four independent LoRA-SFT runs, and the pre-registered estimator replicates on Llama-3.1-8B with Llama-Scope. At 29 steps each, four SFT and four DPO runs occupy disjoint displacement ranges, a gap $5.0\times$ and $5.6\times$ the spreads of the two arms, without any band. All bands use graphs built on one GPU architecture. Three other readings stop short of a verdict: DPO band counts (3/4, 1/4, 3/4 contrasts outside at 12, 29, 60 steps) show no consistent dose ordering while its displacement stays flat; light GRPO is a secondary non-detection, not an absence, and we run no equivalence test; and the graph concentration read as an RL signature is already in the zero-RL base. A pre-registered causal sweep at three edge budgets on 4,816 held-out prompts found the displaced sources load-bearing ($12\times$ a matched random control in mean, $3.5\times$ in median at 10,000 edges), but their stage-specificity interaction reversed at two budgets and the third failed its faithfulness gate, so we claim calibrated structural displacement, not a changed mechanism. Scope: one backbone, one SFT recipe and general-text probe states for the full protocol.</p></details> |  |

| **[Match the Canvas, Not Just the Answer: Predictability-Aware SFT for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18001v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models (DLMs) enable flexible parallel generation by resolving tokens according to the model's evolving predictability rather than a fixed left-to-right order. Standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) typically makes these choices through random masking, independently of inference. Our analysis shows how this training-inference mismatch can misdirect supervision. Random masking can conceal prediction errors or penalize uncertainty that decoding would otherwise resolve, while the attention-visible input length can change which response formulations the model favors, even when the underlying solution is unchanged. Motivated by these findings, we propose Predictability-Aware Canvas Selection and Supervision (PACS), an SFT method. PACS uses the model's own predictability to select each example's attention-visible input length and supervises reference tokens immediately before their confidence-guided revelation. Across reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, PACS consistently achieves the highest mean accuracy among the compared SFT methods, with gains of up to 12.84 percentage points over vanilla SFT on individual benchmarks. Gains grow with model size from 1.5B to 14B. Resources: Qwen3.5-4B compact-backbone release: https://huggingface.co/YueLinHu/GAC-Qwen3.5-4B; reference implementation: https://github.com/deepnovacore/GAC; GAC project page: https://deepnovacore.github.io/GAC/</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 3 figures, 22 tables. | **[What Should SFT Learn? Conditional Information Gain for Generalizable Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12995v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) remains a widely used approach for post-training large language models, because fixed demonstrations can directly introduce desired reasoning strategies and behavioral patterns. Yet forcing a model to imitate externally provided trajectories may also interfere with behaviors already supported by pretraining, limiting generalization beyond the training distribution. Recent dynamic SFT methods mitigate this issue by reweighting token-level supervision according to model-side signals such as predictive probability, prior support, or distributional compatibility. However, model compatibility is not equivalent to learning value: a token that is weakly supported by the current model may either be an undesirable mismatch or precisely the task-specific decision that the demonstration is intended to teach. We propose CIG-SFT, an information-theoretic framework that estimates the task-conditioned information carried by each demonstrated token. Under teacher forcing, we compare the token likelihood from a frozen reference model with and without access to the task input while keeping the gold solution prefix fixed. The resulting conditional information gain quantifies how much the task itself reduces uncertainty about the demonstrated decision. We then convert this information into a KL-regularized projection of the current policy and derive an equivalent scalar-weighted SFT objective whose logit gradient exactly matches that of the projected target. Across mathematical reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, CIG-SFT consistently outperforms standard SFT and recent adaptive fine-tuning baselines, with particularly strong improvements in generalization to challenging evaluation distributions, while requiring neither additional training data nor online rollouts.</p></details> |  |

Across 19 parent-SFT model pairs and 11 benchmarks, SFT-as-context remains close to the SFT models on fine-tuned capabilities, with gaps of only 2.2 and 2.1 percentage points on AIME 2024 and LiveCodeBench and 2.0 macro MAE on NutriBench-English, while staying within 2.2 percentage points of the parent models on general capabilities on average. Remarkably, it can solve queries requiring both fine-tuned and general capabilities, even when neither the parent nor SFT model succeeds alone. This approach also extends beyond parent-SFT pairs: responses from a small open-source SFT model can improve a strong closed-source LLM, outperforming either model alone. Furthermore, we use a Bayesian framework to derive theoretical guarantees that bound the error of SFT-as-context relative to the SFT model on fine-tuned capabilities and to the parent model on general capabilities. In addition, we visualize the attention weights and find that the parent model attends more to useful SFT responses and less to irrelevant ones, suggesting that selective attention helps the parent model use the SFT response through in-context learning.</p></details> | 37 pages |
| **[Train4Merge: A Controlled Single-Teacher Study of RL vs. SFT Teachers for OPD-Based Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32303v2)** | 2026-10-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Domain experts trained from a shared checkpoint can transfer their specialized capabilities to a single student through on-policy distillation (OPD). In Agentic, the best SFT-guided student recovers only 44.44% of its teacher's performance gain over the base model, whereas the best RL-guided student recovers 115.00%, surpassing its teacher. | **[Harmful SFT Leaves a Continuous Trace in LLM Checkpoint Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07518v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safety auditing of post-trained large language models typically relies on model behavior, requiring model execution and depending on the coverage of available evaluations. | **[RT-SFT: Text Style Transfer from Non-Parallel Corpora by Roundtrip Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15013v2)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text style transfer (TST) is naturally a supervised task - rewrite a sentence in a target style while preserving its meaning - yet the parallel corpora that supervision requires exist for only a handful of style domains. | **[mSFT: Addressing Dataset Mixtures Overfitting Heterogeneously in Multi-task SFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21606v6)** | 2026-09-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current language model training commonly applies multi-task Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) using a homogeneous compute budget across all sub-datasets. ## RLHF
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[CleanRM: Reward Modeling from Noisy Feedback with a Few Clean Feedback for RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21138v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feedback noise is pervasive in preference data, arising from subjective human judgments, annotation mistakes, and imperfect LLM judges. Standard reward modeling trained on such feedback yields a biased estimate of the clean risk. Although classical denoising methods are directly applicable to noisy feedback learning, they generally assume a uniform noise ratio across samples. However, this assumption fails for feedback data, where noise is intrinsically sample-wise. To address this challenge, we propose CleanRM, which uses a few clean feedback to calibrate sample-wise noise ratios via optimal transport. Specifically, CleanRM aligns the joint distribution of preferences reverted from noisy feedback with that of clean feedback and then trains the reward model using a noise-corrected surrogate loss. Theoretically, we prove that the resulting objective is unbiased with respect to the clean risk. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CleanRM outperforms state-of-the-art denoising baselines and yields more effective downstream RLHF alignment.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rater State Bias in RLHF Preference Data: An Audit Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16195v3)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We identify a structured confound in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). Pairwise preference labels are intended to reflect the compared outputs, but they may also reflect the rater's state during annotation. Under sustained stressful or distressing conditions, raters' preferences may shift over time, so that preference data encode rater state alongside judgments about response quality. We argue that, if present, such shifts would differ from ordinary disagreement or random label noise. They would be state dependent, could be shared across annotators under similar conditions, and would not necessarily cancel during aggregation, reward modeling, and policy optimization. We propose rater state shift as a plausible and testable source of structured bias in RLHF preference data. This paper develops a hypothesis and an audit framework for studying this source of bias. We define rater state shift, rater state confound, and correlated rater state bias. We also propose survival level emotional authenticity as a candidate output signature, defined by lexical, pragmatic, discourse, and safety features whose reliability and validity remain to be demonstrated. We show that systematic rater state bias can survive aggregation and may enter the learned reward signal. We state five testable predictions, together with effect size thresholds for an initial audit, and note which require proprietary data. Finally, we present an audit protocol and pilot study plan that can be applied to publicly available instruction tuned models. We do not infer the training history of any specific deployed model.</p></details> | <details><summary>v3: R...</summary><p>v3: Refined the mathematical analysis of bias propagation and policy optimization. Extended the discussion to model judges and the possible transmission of preference bias across models. Minor revisions throughout</p></details> |
| **[Reward Shaping to Mitigate Reward Hacking in RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18770v8)** | 2026-09-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) is widely used to align large language models (LLMs) with human preferences. However, RLHF remains vulnerable to \emph{reward hacking}, whereby a policy exploits imperfections in the reward function instead of learning the intended behavior, thereby undermining alignment. Although reward shaping can stabilize RLHF training and partially mitigate reward hacking, shaping methods and their underlying design principles have not been systematically investigated. To address this gap, we conduct a comprehensive study of prevalent reward-shaping techniques. Our analysis identifies two key design principles: (1) the reinforcement-learning reward should be bounded, and (2) it should grow rapidly at first and then gradually saturate. Motivated by these principles, we propose Preference as Reward (PAR), a novel method that uses the latent preferences encoded in the reward model as the reinforcement-learning signal. We further show that PAR possesses two variance-reduction properties that stabilize RLHF training and substantially widen the practical window for early stopping. Our evaluation consists of two parts. First, we compare PAR with several other reward-shaping strategies using Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) as the reinforcement-learning algorithm and Gemma2-2B as the base model. Second, we compare PAR with the vanilla baseline (i.e., unshaped reward) across four base models and four reinforcement-learning algorithms. In the first set of experiments, PAR consistently outperforms other reward-shaping methods and also reflects high data efficiency and robustness. The second set of experiments shows that PAR is particularly effective for actor-critic RL algorithms when value estimates become unstable and demonstrates its effectiveness across different base models. The code is available at https://github.com/PorUna-byte/PAR.</p></details> |  |
| **[Distortion of AI Alignment Revisited: RLHF is a Decent Utilitarian Aligner](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12651v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is the standard paradigm for aligning large language models with human preferences, its effectiveness in pluralistic settings has been called into question. Notably, recent work by Gölz et al. (2025) demonstrated that the \textit{distortion} -- defined as the multiplicative gap between the average user utility of the RLHF policy and the optimal average utility -- can scale exponentially with the Bradley-Terry temperature parameter $β$ when users have heterogeneous preferences. In this work, we present a fine-grained analysis of the distortion of RLHF with reward clipping and demonstrate that such exponential degradation is not a fundamental property of the algorithm but rather a consequence of distribution mismatch between the distribution generating preference data ($μ$) and the KL reference policy ($π_{\mathrm{ref}}$). To this end, we establish tight upper and lower bounds on the distortion of RLHF across multiple regimes of the KL regularization strength. We show that in a representative regime, under the Bradley-Terry model, the distortion is $\tildeΘ(βB + β)$, where $B$ is an upper bound on the log density ratio between $μ$ and $π_{\mathrm{ref}}$. But scoring and generation fight over the same CPU and GPU resources, so a faster scoring engine doesn't shrink step time on its own. It mainly frees up capacity generation can use instead. We built a native C++ inference engine on ONNX Runtime. First step: confirm correctness. Output matched the PyTorch reference to 5.7 x 10^-6 on CPU and 4.2 x 10^-3 on GPU, close enough to trust. Then we tested it against PyTorch eager mode, torch.compile, and FastAPI, on both CPU and GPU. CPU was decisive. Our engine beat every baseline, confidence intervals didn't even overlap. GPU gave a different view: we beat PyTorch and FastAPI, but torch.compile came out ahead. Further testing traced the speedup to ONNX Runtime itself, not C++ as a language. And batching strategy mattered more than either the language or the runtime choice, more than we expected. The results are from repeated, independent runs, since single runs just aren't reliable enough to trust.</p></details> |  |

Experiments on both physical testbeds and a large-scale simulated cluster show that RLHFless achieves up to 1.35x speedup and 44.8% cost reduction compared to the state-of-the-art baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[Generalisation of RLHF under Reward Shift and Clipped KL Regularisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.21765v1)** | 2026-02-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Alignment and adaptation in large language models heavily rely on reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF); yet, theoretical understanding of its generalisability remains premature, especially when the learned reward could shift, and the KL control is estimated and clipped. To address this issue, we develop generalisation theory for RLHF that explicitly accounts for (1) \emph{reward shift}: reward models are trained on preference data from earlier or mixed behaviour policies while RLHF optimises the current policy on its own rollouts; and (2) \emph{clipped KL regularisation}: the KL regulariser is estimated from sampled log-probability ratios and then clipped for stabilisation, resulting in an error to RLHF. We present generalisation bounds for RLHF, suggesting that the generalisation error stems from a sampling error from prompts and rollouts, a reward shift error, and a KL clipping error. We also discuss special cases of (1) initialising RLHF parameters with a uniform prior over a finite space, and (2) training RLHF by stochastic gradient descent, as an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process. The theory yields practical implications in (1) optimal KL clipping threshold, and (2) budget allocation in prompts, rollouts, and preference data.</p></details> |  |
| **[General Exploratory Bonus for Optimistic Exploration in RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03269v4)** | 2026-02-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Optimistic exploration is central to improving sample efficiency in reinforcement learning with human feedback, yet existing exploratory bonus methods to incentivize exploration often fail to realize optimism. We provide a theoretical analysis showing that current formulations, under KL or $α$-divergence regularization, unintentionally bias exploration toward high-probability regions of the reference model, thereby reinforcing conservative behavior instead of promoting discovery of uncertain regions. To address this pitfall, we introduce the General Exploratory Bonus (GEB), a novel theoretical framework that provably satisfies the optimism principle. GEB counteracts divergence-induced bias via reference-dependent reward regulation and unifies prior heuristic bonuses as special cases, while extending naturally across the full $α$-divergence family. Empirically, GEB consistently outperforms baselines on alignment tasks across multiple divergence settings and large language model backbones. These results demonstrate that GEB offers both a principled and practical solution for optimistic exploration in RLHF.</p></details> | ICLR 2026 |
| **[GSRM: Generative Speech Reward Model for Speech RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13891v1)** | 2026-02-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in speech language models, such as GPT-4o Voice Mode and Gemini Live, have demonstrated promising speech generation capabilities.
